Eventually, the allure of stimulation fades, and the mind is no longer hijacked.

**Case Studies of Reactive Feeling in Human Cultures**

Certain cultural and spiritual practices vividly illustrate how this reactive layer can be deliberately amplified and bound to saṃsāra:

- **Orgy traditions in some human tribes** (and related ritual practices): These involve group sexual activity, often using hallucinogens or entheogens to relax inhibitions, lower conscientious guard, and heighten sensory contact. The practice floods the reactive layer with intense attraction, excitement, and energetic merging --- creating waves of pleasure-seeking, temporary euphoria, and group bonding. Yet it generates enormous craving: desire for repetition, escalation, novelty, and the sense of "oneness" through multiplicity. When frustrated (exclusion, jealousy, depletion), aversion and anger surge. Gautama, the Supreme Arahant would point to this as a clear example of phassa-nutriment fueling reactive feeling, leading to bondage in sensual desire and repeated becoming. Such traditions are often influenced by nāga (serpent-being) and draco (reptilian) cultures that emphasize indulgence in group ecstasy as a path to power and pleasure --- reinforcing rather than transcending the cycle.

- **Tantric practices involving male-female energy exchange**: In certain tantric lineages (both human and those involving astral beings), direct energy exchange --- through sexual intercourse, non-physical union, or visualized merging --- is used to generate tremendous pleasure, joy, and ecstatic feelings. The reactive layer is deliberately intensified: attraction to the partner\'s energy, repulsion from separation or blockage, agitation in the buildup and release. The resulting bliss can feel like profoundly spiritual or transcendent, yet it remains rooted in phassa --- contact between subtle bodies or energies --- and produces powerful craving for continuation, mastery, or higher union. This creates bondage to sensual desire, even when framed as "spiritual." Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's teaching on sense restraint and the danger in contact applies directly: such practices, while pleasurable, keep the mind bound to reactive feeling and the nutriments of saṃsāra unless the practitioner sees through to cessation.

**Liberation Approach at This Layer**

The practitioner does not reject contact or suppress reactive surges --- that would create new aversion. Instead, through guarding the sense doors (indriya-saṃvara) and Vedana-anupassanā, reactive feeling is observed directly as it is:

- arising with contact-nutriment

- energetic attraction/repulsion/agitation

- impermanent --- surging, peaking, fading

- conditioned, not true self, not refuge

- the pivot where craving is born or cut

When the energetic leaning is seen without following it --- neither indulging attraction nor fighting repulsion --- the chain weakens. Excitement loses its pull, anxiety its grip, restlessness its churn. The mind begins to relate to contact as a field of insight rather than a source of gratification or threat.

Higher seeing at this level reveals:

- Reactive feeling is the direct outcome of contact-nutriment.

- It conditions the strongest immediate leaning toward or away in saṃsāra.

- Yet it is conditioned, impermanent, and impersonal --- never a source of true stability or happiness.

- The true citta --- luminous at its core, deathless-capable --- is never bound to these energetic surges; it uses restraint and clear seeing to loosen dependence and prepare for independence (anissito).

The practitioner abides in the field of reactive feeling, seeing its directional surges without leaning toward or pushing away. As understanding matures here, identification with excitement, anxiety, or restlessness as "mine" weakens. The door opens naturally to the next layer: emotionalized feeling, where the tone becomes structured and sustained.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 7.5 Level 3 --- Emotionalized Feeling

As Vedana-anupassanā penetrates deeper, the raw energetic surge of Level 2 develops into structured, sustained emotion.

Here, feeling becomes emotion:

- desire

- fear

- anger

- attachment

The tone is no longer momentary or purely directional; it solidifies into lasting patterns, forming emotional structures that imprint on the body, mind, and identity. Emotion is layered --- it builds narratives, memories, and habitual responses that persist across time, turning transient vedanā into a defining force in human experience.

Humans as a race are particularly characterized by intense emotions and emotionalized feeling. This intensity arises from our hybrid nature: modern scientific research reveals that humanity was hybrided from a monkey-like primate ancestor with divine sources. The animal part of our being --- the survival-driven mammalian/reptilian brain --- is the primary source of raw fear and anger instincts. These primal circuits can dominate the entire human existence when unregulated, overwhelming higher faculties with fight-flight-freeze responses. Anger, in particular, stands out as one of the most powerful emotions humans possess --- it mobilizes enormous energy, overrides reason, and can propel destructive action with terrifying force.

Contemporary neuroscience underscores this: what human beings experience as "love" is largely a neurobiological phenomenon characterized by the release of dopamine (reward/motivation), oxytocin (bonding/trust), and serotonin (mood stability). These chemicals create the intense pull of affection, attachment, and romantic ecstasy. Anger, likewise, is a neurobiological event --- triggered by threat perception, flooding the system with adrenaline and cortisol, narrowing focus, and readily overwhelming affection or rationality. Love and hate thus form a volatile polarity: both generate huge cravings (taṇhā) and aversion (dosa), propelling beings deeper into saṃsāra through endless cycles of grasping, frustration, and renewed becoming.

Yet humans also possess distinctly elevated capacities that set them apart from purely animal reactivity: **emotional empathy** and **love** as healing forces. Emotional empathy --- the ability to feel another\'s pain or joy as one\'s own --- creates deep bonds among humans, enabling mutual support, compassion, and collective healing. Human love, when expressed through care, protection, and self-giving, can serve as a powerful medicine --- soothing trauma, restoring trust, and fostering growth in relationships. Emotional values between friends --- loyalty, shared vulnerability, encouragement in suffering, rejoicing in success --- are typical human traits that build trust and community far beyond survival instincts.

These qualities are double-edged: when rooted in wisdom and non-clinging, empathy and love become skillful means for alleviating suffering and supporting the path. They can function as genuine healing tools, helping others and oneself move toward stability and clarity. But when unregulated or clung to --- when empathy turns into emotional fusion, or love into possessive attachment --- they generate enormous bondage. The mind becomes entangled in others\' feelings, fears loss or rejection, creates dependency, and fuels craving for reciprocity or validation. Thus, even these elevated human traits can propel saṃsāra when they feed identification, expectation, and subtle clinging.

In Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's profound analysis of existence, this emotionalized layer is most directly nourished and sustained by the third nutriment: **manosañ-cetana-ahāra** --- mental volition or intention (cetanā).

Gautama, the Supreme Arahant declares: "Cetanāhaṃ, bhikkhave, kammaṃ vadāmi --- Monks, I call volition karma" (AN 6.63). Volition is the seed of action --- the intentional energy that shapes thought, speech, and deed, propelling becoming (bhava) and rebirth. We are constantly feeding the future through our intentions --- desiring, projecting, planning, fearing, resenting, clinging. This subtle but potent fuel sustains the churning of saṅkhāras (mental formations), turning emotionalized feeling into karmic momentum that binds citta to conditioned existence.

The emotionalized layer thrives on this nutriment: desire volitions propel attachment and pursuit; fear volitions generate avoidance and contraction; anger volitions fuel destruction and defense; attachment volitions reinforce identity and possession. Each intentional movement feeds the next emotional wave, creating self-reinforcing patterns that define "who I am" in the world.

**Liberation Approach at This Layer**

The path is not to suppress intention or emotion --- suppression creates new aversion and hidden volition. Instead, the practitioner purifies intention through sammā-saṅkappa (right intention): renunciation (nekkhamma), non-cruelty (avihiṃsā), and harmlessness.

Through meditative stillness (samādhi) and vedanānupassanā, the churning of emotionalized volition is observed directly:

- arising with mental nutriment

- structured as desire/fear/anger/attachment

- impermanent --- building, sustaining, fading

- conditioned, not true self, not refuge

- the engine of karma and becoming

Empathy and love, when purified, become expressions of mettā and karuṇā --- active goodwill and compassionate response without clinging or fusion. Emotional values between friends are transformed into muditā (rejoicing in others\' good) and upekkhā (transcendental equanimity that supports without entanglement). When seen clearly and not grasped, these human strengths cease to generate bondage and instead support the path toward liberation.

Higher seeing at this level reveals:

- Emotionalized feeling is the direct outcome of mental-volition nutriment.

- It conditions the strongest sustained patterns of craving and aversion in saṃsāra.

- Yet it is conditioned, impermanent, and not true self --- never a source of true stability or happiness.

- The true self of citta --- luminous at its core, deathless-capable --- is never bound to these emotional structures or volitional churnings; it uses right intention and clear seeing to purify volition and prepare for independence (anissito).

The practitioner abides in the field of emotionalized feeling, seeing its structured patterns and volitional roots without grasping desire or recoiling from anger. As understanding matures here, identification with emotion as "me" or "mine" weakens profoundly. The door opens naturally to the next layer: conceptualized feeling, where the tone becomes interpreted and narrated.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 7.6 Level 4 --- Conceptualized Feeling

As Vedana-anupassanā matures and emotionalized patterns are seen clearly, the tone of experience enters the conceptualized layer.

At this level:

- feeling is interpreted

- meaning is assigned

- value judgments and stories are overlaid

Examples include:

- "this is good"

- "this is bad"

- "this is mine"

- "this means I am loved / rejected / safe / threatened / worthy / unworthy"

Feeling is now linked with:

- thought

- belief

- narrative

This linking reinforces identity. The raw tone (pleasant/unpleasant/neutral) is no longer simply felt --- it is woven into a conceptual framework that defines "who I am" in relation to the world (such as the feeling of "I am offended.").

**Formation of Self-Identity from Life Experience**

From a scientific and psychological perspective, self-identity at this layer is constructed through accumulated life experience. Early interactions with caregivers, peers, culture, and environment create core schemas --- mental models of self, others, and the world. Positive reinforcement (praise, affection) builds narratives of worthiness; trauma, rejection, or failure imprints narratives of defectiveness or danger. These schemas operate largely unconsciously, filtering all subsequent experience: a neutral event is interpreted as "proof" of an existing belief ("people always leave me"), strengthening the conceptual self. Neuroplasticity shows how repeated patterns strengthen neural pathways, making these identities feel solid and real. Yet they remain conditioned constructs --- maps, not the territory --- shaped by environment, conditioning, and survival needs.

Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's teaching on anattā (not-self) directly addresses this: the self-concept formed from worldly experience is the bondage that propels endless saṃsāra. There is no permanent, independent self within the five worldly aggregates (form, feeling, perception, formations, consciousness). What we call such "I" or "me" is a false self --- a conceptual fabrication (sakkāya-diṭṭhi, the body related view, the worldly senses generated consciousness) arising from clinging to these aggregates as "mine." This false self is the eating bird in the ancient Two Birds archetype from the Vedas, reinterpreted through Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's lens:

**The Two Birds --- An Archetype of the Soul**

The Veda spoke of two birds upon the same tree. The tree is the body, and by extension, the world (loka) itself.

- The eating bird is the **false self** --- the worldly ego (ahaṅkāra). It consumes the fruit of existence, drawn by sweetness (pleasant feeling), recoiling from bitterness (unpleasant feeling), never satisfied. It survives by feeding on the three poisons: craving (rāga) for continuity and pleasure, aversion (dosa) for defense against threats, and ignorance (moha) --- the deepest forgetting of the manussa spark's true home. Forgetting Nibbāna-dhātu, the spark identifies with the tree, the fruit, the shadow-bird itself, or even mistakes dissolution into a cosmic Source for final destiny. The eating bird exists because survival in this cosmos requires such a garment. Without it, the true spark could not remain here. Yet what once protected now imprisons.

- The watching bird is the **true self** --- the ātman, the manussa spark, luminous and eternal. It does not eat, for it belongs not to the cycle of hunger and decay. It abides in the unconditioned (asaṅkhata), deathless by nature, though obscured by the restless feeding of its companion. The true bird is never defiled, but while lodged in the same tree, it is bound, waiting for release.

The two birds are companions, but not equals. The eating bird is temporal, woven of survival instincts. The watching bird is eternal, untouched, remembering the sky.

**Epigraph: Ṛg Veda 1.164.37**

**Transliteration:**\
*dvā suparṇā sayujā sakhāyā samānaṃ vṛkṣaṃ pari ṣasvajāte /\
tayor anyaḥ pippalaṃ svādv atty an-aśnann anyo abhicākaśīti //*

**Literal Translation:**\
"Two birds, united companions, cling to the same tree.\
One of them eats the sweet fruit; the other looks on without eating."

![Generated image](media/image11.jpeg){width="3.012820428696413in" height="4.5192311898512685in"}

**Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's Path in the Language of Birds**

Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's teaching is the call to distinguish the birds. Not to confuse the consuming bird with the true self. Not to polish the false bird with ornaments of philosophy or ritual. Instead:

- To let the consuming bird die --- not by hatred, but by starving it of its fuel.

- To burn away craving, aversion, and ignorance through sīla (virtue), samādhi (concentration), and paññā (wisdom).

- To awaken the true self from forgetfulness, so it remembers its true home in Nibbāna-dhātu.

Gautama, the Supreme Arahant did not create a new path --- he uncovered the exit. His path is the gradual release of the higher bird from its prison-companion, until at last it takes flight beyond the tree, beyond the cosmos.

**The Subtlest Nutriment: Worldly Consciousness (Viññāṇa-ahāra)**

The fourth and most profound nutriment is worldly consciousness itself (viññāṇa). It is called "worldly" because it originates from the five external senses plus the mind's internal mental activities --- the six types of consciousness:

1.  Eye-consciousness (cakkhu-viññāṇa): Arises when eye meets visual form.

2.  Ear-consciousness (sota-viññāṇa): Arises when ear meets sound.

3.  Nose-consciousness (ghāna-viññāṇa): Arises when nose meets odor.

4.  Tongue-consciousness (jivhā-viññāṇa): Arises when tongue meets flavor.

5.  Body-consciousness (kāya-viññāṇa): Arises when body meets tactile object.

6.  Mind-consciousness (mano-viññāṇa): Arises with thoughts, ideas, or mental phenomena.

Just as a fire burns dependent on fuel, worldly consciousness arises dependent on name-and-form (nāma-rūpa) and in turn conditions rebirth in this world. In the simile of the leper (SN 12.63), Gautama, the Supreme Arahant describes this consciousness feeding on its objects, ever hungry. This is the deepest trap: worldly viññāṇa sustains the illusion of a false self-identity by its constant engagement with the six sense spheres.

**Liberation Approach at This Layer**

The practitioner does not reject conceptualization or consciousness --- rejection creates new aversion. Instead, through guarding the sense doors, meditative stillness (samādhi), and higher seeing (anupassanā / vipassanā), the impermanent, conditioned, and not-true-self nature of worldly consciousness is penetrated.

Concentration practices such as kāyagatāsati (mindfulness irected to the body) and ānāpānasati (mindfulness of breathing) withdraw awareness from the six sense doors, allowing citta to abide in luminous inward stillness. With insight, the mind sees:

- Conceptualized feeling arises with mental nutriment and worldly consciousness.

- It reinforces the false self through interpretation, narrative, and identity-clinging.

- Yet it is conditioned, impermanent, and not-true-self.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 7.7 Level 5 --- Refined Feeling (Brahmanic Layer)

At higher refinement of citta, feeling becomes calm, expansive, and balanced --- no longer driven by personal craving or aversion, but aligned with higher, value-based qualities.

This includes:

- mettā (kindness) --- pure goodwill extended without attachment or expectation

- karuṇā (compassion) --- active response to suffering without domination or pity

- muditā (rejoicing in manussa) --- Brahmic joy that celebrates the virtue, success, and welfare of manussa, the race of man

- upekkhā (transcendental liberation) --- aligned with transcendental liberation, steady balance when dealing with human affairs that stands beyond favor, fear, aversion, or partiality

These are refined, value-aligned feeling states. They stabilize the mind, reduce defilement, and create inner spaciousness. They are divine feelings --- resonant with the qualities of the Brahmā realms.

This layer is especially important because the Brahmā gods represent one of the most significant divine sources influencing human existence. They embody and transmit the higher values that shape human society --- justice, fairness, kindness, truth-seeking, harmony without domination, and the recognition of intrinsic worth in every conscious being. These values provide essential group support for Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's liberation path. Without this Brahma-aligned resonance, the path can become isolated, harsh, or distorted by lower instincts.

The canonical story of Brahmā Sahampati illustrates this directly. After Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's awakening under the Bodhi tree, he realized the true condition of humanity --- the profound challenges and entanglements humans face on earth, trapped in cycles of manipulation, hybrid influences, flawed existence, and deep conditioning. Recognizing how difficult it would be for most to understand and follow the path to liberation, he initially hesitated to teach. At that moment, Brahmā Sahampati, perceiving Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's thought with his divine eye, appeared radiating great light. He bowed before the Awakened One and spoke:

"Santi sattā apparajakkhajātikā... There are beings with little dust in their eyes. If they do not hear the Dhamma, they will perish. Some will understand."

Moved by this plea, Gautama's compassion deepened into mahā-karuṇā --- great compassion that embraces even those most deeply caught. Though the task was immense, he resolved to teach the path that leads true liberation: Nibbidā → Virāga → Vimutti → Nibbāna-dhātu.

Our practices point toward this Brahma resonance:

- Brahmacariya (the holy life): celibate life, withdraws from sensual craving and worldly entanglement, aligning citta with purity and restraint.

- Brahmavihāra (divine abidings): cultivate the refined feelings themselves, turning the mind toward Brahma-like qualities.

Non-returners (anāgāmī) and arahants often take rebirth in the five Pure Abodes (Suddhāvāsa) --- the highest Brahma heavens in the rūpa realm --- before final liberation. These realms are pure, free from sensuality, and dedicated to the completion of the path. Thus, Brahma-aligned divine identity and refined feeling serve as a vital bridge --- stabilizing and uplifting citta until the final departure to Nibbāna-dhātu.

Gautama, the Supreme Arahant repeatedly identified himself with this Brahma lineage, declaring in the Pali Canon:

"Ahamasmi, bhikkhave, brāhmaṇo yācayogo sadā payatapāṇi antimadehadharo anuttaro bhisakko sallakatto. Tassa me tumhe puttā orasā mukhato jātā dhammajā dhammanimmitā dhammadāyādā, no āmisadāyādā."

"Bhikkhus, I am a brāhmaṇa, committed to charity, always open-handed, bearing my final body, a healer, a surgeon. You are my rightful children, born of my mouth, born of my Dhamma, created by my Dhamma, heirs in my Dhamma, not in material things." (Itivuttaka 4.100, Brāhmaṇa-dhamma-yāga Sutta)

In Dhammapada, the entire Brāhmaṇa Vagga (Chapter 26) celebrates this noble identity:

"Chinda sotaṃ parakkamma, kāme panuda brāhmaṇa; Saṅkhārānaṃ khayaṃ ñatvā, akataññū'si brāhmaṇa." (383)

"Striving, cut off the stream, drive away sensual pleasures, O brāhmaṇa; Having known the destruction of formations, you are one who knows the uncreated, O brāhmaṇa."

"Yadā dvayesu dhammesu, pāragū hoti brāhmaṇo; Atha'ssa sabbe saṃyogā, atthaṃ gacchanti jānato." (384)

"When a brāhmaṇa has gone to the other shore in regard to two dhammas, Then all his bonds go to an end, for the one who knows."

"Yassa pāraṃ apāraṃ vā, pārāpāraṃ vā vijjati; Vītaddaraṃ visaṃyuttaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi brāhmaṇaṃ." (385)

"He who knows this shore and the other shore, or both shores, Who is free from craving and unbound --- him I call a brāhmaṇa."

By reinforcing this Brahma-aligned divine identity and cultivating these refined feelings, the practitioner progresses on the liberation path. The divine feelings stabilize citta, reduce defilement, and create the inner spaciousness needed for deeper insight. They align the practitioner with the noble lineage --- the Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's children born of Dhamma --- preparing citta for independence from the world (anissito) and final departure to Nibbāna-dhātu.

Higher seeing at this level reveals:

- Refined (Brahmanic) feeling arises when citta resonates with value-aligned sources.

- It stabilizes and purifies, serving as essential support until release.

- Yet it is still conditioned, arising within loka --- never the final refuge.

- The true citta --- luminous at its core, deathless-capable --- uses these divine tones as a bridge, not a home; it progresses through them toward complete non-dependence and realization of the Deathless.

The practitioner abides in the field of refined feeling, seeing its calm, expansive, divine qualities that leads to final attainment. As understanding matures here, even attachment to divine stability weakens. The door opens naturally to the final layer: subtle stillness (near-neutral feeling), where tone fades into luminous quietude at the threshold of transcendence.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 7.8 Level 6 --- Subtle Stillness (Near-Neutral Feeling)

At the highest conditioned level of vedanā, feeling becomes extremely subtle, almost neutral, and barely perceptible.

This is experienced as:

- deep stillness

- quiet presence

- luminous calm that permeates without intensity

- profound bliss that arises not from sensory contact or gratification, but from the mind\'s resonance with qualities beyond the world

There is:

- no strong pleasure to grasp

- no disturbance to resist

- only the barest trace of tone, fading into luminous quietude suffused with subtle, non-sensual bliss

This layer arises naturally when citta has been purified through the preceding levels: gross sensory reactivity calmed, energetic leaning restrained, emotional patterns dissolved, conceptual overlays seen through, and refined Brahmanic tones stabilized without clinging. What remains is a near-neutral field --- the last whisper of feeling before the boundary of conditioned experience itself.

This subtle stillness and its accompanying bliss are close to Nibbāna-dhātu. They exhibit great peace and a direct reflection of Upasama: the Ultimate Peace, the supreme quality of Nibbāna-dhātu. As citta approaches this threshold, it begins to resonate with the inherent qualities of the Deathless realm itself:

- **Sublime (Paṇīta)** --- the feeling becomes sublime, surpassing all worldly pleasures in its refined elevation and purity.

- **Auspicious (Siva)** --- it carries an auspicious quality, bringing deep tranquility and well-being that flows from alignment with higher reality.

- **Haven (Khema)** --- it feels like a safe haven, free from danger, fear, or affliction --- a secure refuge that foreshadows the ultimate safety of Nibbāna.

- **Blissful (Sugata)** --- non-sensual bliss permeates the stillness, joy and contentment that transcend all conditioned gratification, reflecting the supreme happiness of the Deathless.

- **Unafflicted (Abyābajjha)** --- no trace of suffering or affliction remains; the field is complete peace, liberated from all disturbance.

This is true freedom in reflection: the complete overcoming of the suffering inherent in worldly aging and death begins to be tasted here, pointing toward an eternal, deathless existence suffused with profound peace and bliss. Far from nihilism or mere annihilation, Nibbāna-dhātu is a real, transcendent realm of existence that lies beyond the conditioned worlds, beyond even the limits of the universe itself --- the final destination and true home of the luminous citta.

Gautama, the Supreme Arahant's teaching points to three progressive layers of true peace that unfold on the path, each building toward this ultimate reflection:

1.  **Inner Peace (The Root)** Purifying the mind through meditation. As Gautama, the Supreme Arahant taught: "If we are peaceful on the inside, we will be peaceful on the outside." This inner stillness is the foundation --- when citta is no longer agitated by greed, hatred, or delusion, it naturally radiates calm regardless of external conditions.

2.  **Peace Between Beings** Extending mettā and the brahmavihāra to all, recognizing shared vulnerability to impermanence and affirming our common humanity. This counters division, hatred, and separation by affirming value-aligned connection. Collective peace among humans arises not from treaties or absence of war (which remain illusory while greed, hatred, and fear persist in hearts), but from individuals achieving inner peace. As Gautama, the Supreme Arahant taught, lasting harmony follows naturally when enough beings cultivate this inner root.

3.  **Peace with the World (Disenchantment)** Accepting the world's instability without attachment or aversion. Through embracing simplicity, non-clinging, and nibbidā (disenchantment), the practitioner prepares citta to transcend worldly cycles entirely. This peace is not resignation --- it is the mature recognition that no conditioned phenomenon can provide lasting refuge. It points directly toward the ultimate peace: Nibbāna-dhātu, the deathless realm of existence.

This subtle stillness and bliss is the conditioned reflection of that ultimate Upasama --- the True Peace. It is the quietude and joy that arise when all strong tones have been seen and released, when citta no longer leans on any feeling for support. Here, the mind stands at the threshold: still within loka, yet resonant with qualities that belong to the Deathless.

Higher seeing at this level reveals:

- Subtle stillness (near-neutral feeling) arises as the natural culmination of refinement.

- It exhibits great peace and non-sensual bliss --- a reflection of Nibbāna-dhātu's supreme upasama (stilling), paṇīta (sublimity), siva (auspiciousness), khema (haven), sugata (bliss), and abyābajjha (unafflictedness).

- Yet it is still conditioned, arising within loka --- never the final reality itself.

- The true citta --- luminous at its core, deathless-capable --- uses this near-neutral quietude and bliss as the last bridge, not a home; it progresses through it toward complete independence (anissito) and direct realization of Nibbāna-dhātu.

The practitioner abides in this subtle stillness, seeing its deep quiet presence and luminous bliss without grasping the calm or seeking to prolong it. As understanding matures here, even the subtlest dependence on any tone of feeling falls away completely. Identification with stillness or bliss as "mine" or attainment dissolves entirely. The conditioned layers of vedanā --- from gross sensory to this near-neutral fading --- have been seen in their totality as impermanent, conditioned, and incapable of providing lasting refuge.

From this threshold, the path reaches its natural conclusion for feeling: citta, fully purified and independent, departs the world of tone and contact entirely. No new craving arises. The Deathless realm --- Amata (Deathless), Ajara (Ageless), Dhuva (Stable), Sukha (Blissful beyond sense), Suddha (Pure), Santi (Peaceful) --- is realized as the true existence beyond all worlds.

This completes the six layers of vedanā. Through vedanānupassanā, the practitioner has seen feeling in feelings from gross sensory to subtle stillness --- not to refine or glorify experience, but to understand it so completely that citta stands free, unbound, and prepared for final release.

### 7.9 Closing Reflection 

The practitioner begins with feeling as raw bodily tone. Through direct higher seeing (Vedana-anupassanā): it becomes energetic attraction/repulsion → structured emotion → interpreted narrative → refined divine alignment → subtle stillness suffused with bliss. At every layer, dependence on the worldly weakens. At the final threshold, tone fades into luminous quietude and non-sensual bliss. Citta, luminous and deathless-capable, no longer leans on any residue of feeling. From this independence: liberation is ready. The world loses its tonal jurisdiction. The Deathless is known.

Yet along this path, major obstacles arise, especially with pleasant feeling. The mind tends to grasp it, prolong it, and identify with it --- whether coarse pleasure, refined calm, or meditative bliss. Without clear seeing, pleasant feeling becomes a subtle form of bondage, drawing citta back into craving even at higher levels.

Unpleasant feeling triggers resistance, rejection, and avoidance --- creating tension, contraction, and suffering. Through Vedana-anupassanā, the practitioner sees unpleasant feeling simply as tone, not as problem --- allowing resistance to dissolve naturally.

Neutral feeling often goes unnoticed, yet it supports dullness, inattention, and ignorance. When clearly seen, even neutral feeling becomes an object of insight --- revealing its conditioned nature and breaking the veil of moha.

The chain of conditioning is: contact → feeling → reaction → attachment. By seeing feeling directly in the moment of its arising --- before reaction follows --- the chain is broken at its key turning point.

With practice:

- pleasant feeling arises → no grasping

- unpleasant feeling arises → no resistance

<!-- -->

- neutral feeling arises → no ignorance

This leads to non-reactivity --- the mind no longer pulled or pushed by tone. From non-reactivity arises disenchantment (nibbidā): as all feeling is seen clearly, its instability and limitations become evident. Feeling is no longer trusted as a source of fulfillment or refuge.

From disenchantment:

- attachment fades (virāga)

<!-- -->

- the mind releases (vimutti)

Feeling continues to arise, but it no longer binds.

At advanced stages, feeling becomes extremely subtle, citta becomes stable and clear, and the practitioner may experience deep peace, stillness, and non-sensual well-being. These are refined feeling states --- resonances with higher qualities --- yet they are not final liberation. They remain conditioned, arising within loka.

At higher levels, feeling begins to reflect the qualities of Nibbāna-dhātu:

- peace (Santi)

- purity (Suddha)

- stability (Dhuva)

These are resonances with the Deathless --- glimpses of the supreme peace (upasama) --- yet they are still within the conditioned field.

When fully understood:

- pleasant feeling is not grasped

- unpleasant feeling is not resisted

<!-- -->

- neutral feeling is not ignored

Thus: dependence on feeling ends. This supports anissito --- citta abiding independent from the world, no longer leaning on any tone for identity, stability, or support.

Feeling is subtle, immediate, and powerful. It shapes reaction, identity, and attachment. When seen clearly:

- its hold weakens

- its patterns dissolve

<!-- -->

- its authority fades

From this: the mind becomes free from the pull of experience. And the path moves deeper: from feeling to mind, from mind to liberation.

![](media/image12.jpeg){width="4.545138888888889in" height="3.0319444444444446in"}

## 8. The Six Layers of Citta

***From Reactive Mind to Purified Citta***

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 8.1 Citta as the Central Field --- Mind in Mind

Among the four fields of Satipaṭṭhāna --- kāya (body), vedanā (feeling), citta (mind/heart), and dhammā (phenomena) --- citta is central.

It knows the body. It experiences feeling. It engages with dhammā.

Citta is the seat of knowing, continuity, and orientation across all experience. It is not produced by the body, nor is it a mere byproduct of sensory contact. It is the luminous faculty through which all experience is known --- the aware, radiant core that witnesses, orients, and abides through arising and passing phenomena.

The canonical instruction is precise:

**citte cittānupassī viharati**

"abides higher-seeing mind in mind"

This higher seeing means:

- observing citta directly

- recognizing its states

- seeing transitions in real time

The practitioner does not analyze the mind conceptually, label its movements, or stand apart watching it as an object. Instead, awareness enters the field of citta itself and abides within it --- seeing mind exactly from within the mind, without mediation or division.

Citta is not static. It shifts rapidly:

- from calm to agitation

- from clarity to confusion

- from openness to contraction

- from scattered distraction to collected unification

- from defiled reactivity to luminous purity

Ordinarily, these shifts go unnoticed --- citta flows in habitual patterns, pulled by contact, feeling, and volition, without clear recognition of its own movement. Through sustained citta-anupassanā, the dynamic nature of citta becomes visible. The practitioner directly knows:

- how states arise dependent on conditions

- how they persist or intensify through volition and habit

- how they fade when not fed

- how the luminous core remains untouched beneath every shift

This direct seeing from within reveals citta as neither solid entity nor empty void --- it is the luminous, knowing faculty, deathless-capable when purified, yet ordinarily veiled by reactivity, identification, and worldly leaning. The purpose of cittānupassanā is radical: to see citta so completely that all dependence on conditioned states ceases, allowing the luminous core to abide independent (anissito) from the world and prepare for final departure to Nibbāna-dhātu.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 8.2 The Six Layers of Citta --- Overview

Citta-anupassanā --- higher seeing mind in mind --- naturally discloses six progressive layers of citta. These are not separate minds or metaphysical compartments; they represent deepening levels of refinement and freedom in how the knowing faculty itself is directly known through internal, continuous supreme seeing.

Citta unfolds across six layers:

1.  Sensory-bound citta

2.  Reactive citta

3.  Patterned (habitual) citta

4.  Reflective citta

5.  Collected (unified) citta

6.  Purified citta

These layers correspond directly to the increasing refinement of awareness and mirror the progression seen in the layers of kāya and vedanā. Because citta is the central knowing faculty that experiences body, feeling, and mental phenomena, each layer is shaped by and reflects the conditioning present in the other fields:

- Sensory-bound citta is dominated by external contact and gross bodily input.

- Reactive citta surges with the energetic tone of vedanā (attraction/repulsion).

- Patterned citta carries the emotional imprints and volitional habits from vedanā and dhammā.

- Reflective citta begins to conceptualize and narrate experience, overlaying identity.

- Collected citta unifies and stabilizes, resonating with Brahmanic values and divine alignment.

- Purified citta emerges as the luminous core, free from all defilement and worldly leaning.

Each layer becomes clearly perceptible when attention remains stable, unbroken, and free from reactivity or conceptual overlay. The progression across these layers follows one consistent direction:

from scattered, externally driven reactivity → through habitual patterning and conceptual identification → toward unification and refinement → culminating in complete purification

At every step, identification with citta\'s states as "me" or "mine" weakens. Dependence on conditioned mental states for identity, stability, or support steadily dissolves.

By the time the sixth layer becomes stable in direct experience, citta has been seen in its entirety as the luminous faculty that can stand independent (anissito) from all worldly phenomena --- no longer bound by sensory input, reactive surges, emotional patterns, conceptual narratives, or even refined states. It belongs to the Deathless when fully purified.

This progressive disclosure through higher seeing prepares citta to abide free from the entire field of conditioned knowing and opens the path toward departure to Nibbāna-dhātu, which lies completely beyond the jurisdiction of any mental state or layer.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 8.3 Level 1 --- Sensory-Bound Citta

This is the most immediate and coarse layer of citta --- the level at which the mind is almost entirely dominated by external sensory input and gross bodily contact.

It manifests as:

- attention scattered and pulled outward by sights, sounds, smells, tastes, touches

- awareness unstable, flickering from one stimulus to the next

- knowing fragmented, reactive, and largely automatic

Characteristics include:

- constant distraction and sensory chasing

- impulsive shifts of attention

- strong dependence on external objects for orientation and stability

- minimal inner continuity --- citta is "bound" to the sense doors, rising and falling with each contact

At this layer, citta is not yet capable of sustained inward turning. It functions primarily as a receiver and responder to the world: a pleasant sight draws leaning in, an unpleasant sound provokes recoil, neutral input is ignored or dulled. The luminous core is deeply veiled --- citta feels like a passive mirror reflecting whatever strikes it, with little recognition of its own movement or nature.

Through citta-anupassanā, the practitioner begins to abide inside this sensory-bound field and sees it directly as it is --- without trying to suppress distractions or force focus. Higher seeing reveals:

- every state of mind arises dependent on contact (phassa) with the six sense bases

- attention shifts are conditioned by pleasant/unpleasant/neutral vedanā arising from those contacts

- no stable "I" or controller exists behind the flux --- only conditioned arising and passing

- citta at this level is wholly worldly (loka-bound), incapable of providing refuge or true continuity

As this clear seeing stabilizes:

- the compulsive pull of sensory objects begins to loosen

- the mind starts to recognize its own scattering instead of being lost in it

- identification with "I am distracted" or "I am overwhelmed by the world" weakens slightly

**Comparison with Modern Research**

This sensory-bound state of citta finds striking parallels in several fields of contemporary psychology, neuroscience, and sociology:

- **Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder (OCD)** --- In OCD, the mind becomes trapped in stimulus-bound loops: intrusive sensory or mental stimuli trigger intense anxiety, leading to compulsive checking, washing, or mental rituals. The person is unable to disengage from the sensory or thought input, mirroring how sensory-bound citta reacts automatically to contact without inner freedom or perspective. The difference is that in ordinary sensory-bound citta, the reactivity is subtler and more pervasive, while OCD represents an extreme amplification of this binding.

- **Psychology: "Stimulus-Bound" Behavior** --- Early behavioral and cognitive psychology described "stimulus-bound" individuals as those whose responses are almost entirely determined by immediate environmental cues, with little internal regulation or goal-directed behavior. This matches the description of citta at this layer: attention and action are enslaved to whatever stimulus is strongest, lacking reflective distance or volitional override.

- **Neuroscience: The "Default Mode Network" (DMN)** --- When the mind is not engaged in focused tasks, the DMN activates --- associated with mind-wandering, self-referential thinking, and rumination. In sensory-bound states, however, the DMN is suppressed by constant external input (task-positive network dominance), yet the mind still lacks true inward stability. The DMN\'s overactivity in clinical conditions (depression, anxiety) shows what happens when sensory binding gives way to uncontrolled internal narrative --- but in the untrained mind, sensory dominance prevents even that level of self-reflection, keeping citta externally tethered.

- **Developmental Psychology: "Sensorimotor" Stage** --- Piaget's first stage (birth to \~2 years) describes cognition as entirely sensorimotor: intelligence is bound to immediate perception and action, with no capacity for mental representation or detachment. Sensory-bound citta in adults represents a regression or persistence of this primitive mode --- knowing is limited to the here-and-now of contact, without the symbolic or reflective distance that develops later.

- **Modern Sociology: "Continuous Partial Attention"** --- Linda Stone coined this term to describe the contemporary state of divided, always-on awareness in a digital environment: constantly scanning for new stimuli (notifications, alerts, multitasking), never fully present in any one thing. This is a cultural amplification of sensory-bound citta --- the mind is perpetually pulled outward, fragmented, and reactive, with diminished capacity for sustained inward knowing or continuity.

- **Trauma & Dissociation: "Hyper-Vigilance"** --- In trauma survivors, hyper-vigilance is a chronic state of scanning the environment for threat --- sensory input (sounds, movements, facial expressions) dominates awareness, triggering fight/flight responses even when no danger exists. This mirrors sensory-bound citta but with heightened fear-based reactivity. In both cases, citta is bound to external cues, unable to rest in its own luminous nature or disengage from contact.

Higher seeing at this level reveals:

- Sensory-bound citta is the direct outcome of contact-nutriment and gross vedanā.

- It conditions the strongest immediate reactivity and external dependence in saṃsāra.

- Yet it is conditioned, impermanent, and impersonal --- never a source of true knowing or stability.

- The true citta --- luminous at its core, deathless-capable --- is never identical with this scattered, sensory-driven state; it uses clear seeing to loosen dependence and prepare for independence (anissito).

The practitioner abides in this sensory-bound field, seeing its fragmented, externally-driven movements without grasping distraction or recoiling from overwhelm. As understanding matures here, identification with sensory reactivity as "me" begins to weaken. The door opens naturally to the next layer: reactive citta, where mental states become emotionally charged and directional.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 8.4 Level 2 --- Reactive Citta

As citta-anupassanā deepens and the sensory-bound layer is seen clearly, citta reveals its next mode: the reactive layer. Here, the mind is no longer merely a passive receiver of sensory input; it becomes actively charged, directional, and emotionally responsive.

It manifests as:

- emotional reactivity surging in response to vedanā (pleasant → attraction, unpleasant → aversion, neutral → dullness or restlessness)

- states of agitation, excitement, fear, irritation, or craving

- rapid shifts between expansion (leaning toward) and contraction (pushing away)

Characteristics include:

- strong emotional fluctuation and instability

- mind governed by attraction and resistance

- volitional impulses arising immediately after contact and feeling

- citta pulled outward by desire or pushed inward by fear/anger

At this layer, citta is governed by the push-pull dynamics of vedanā. A pleasant tone ignites leaning in (excitement, desire, attachment); an unpleasant tone triggers pushing away (anxiety, anger, aversion); neutral tone often leads to subtle boredom or inattention. The luminous core remains veiled --- citta feels like a battleground of forces, reacting automatically without clear inner knowing or space for discernment.

Through citta-anupassanā, the practitioner abides inside this reactive field and sees it directly as it is --- without trying to suppress emotions or force calm. Higher seeing reveals:

- reactive states arise dependent on vedanā and contact-nutriment

- attraction and aversion condition volitional leaning (cetanā), planting seeds of karma

- no permanent "I" controls these surges --- only conditioned arising, intensification, and fading

- citta at this level is still wholly worldly, bound to the cycle of push-pull and emotional turbulence

As this clear seeing stabilizes:

- the automatic reactivity begins to slow and become visible

- the mind starts to recognize its own leaning instead of being lost in it

- identification with "I am angry" or "I am excited" weakens slightly

Even at this reactive layer, the practitioner directly understands: reactive citta belongs entirely to the domain of conditioned arising and ceasing. It can never be the luminous, deathless-capable core. It can never serve as the final standing-place or lasting home for the knowing faculty that is capable of purification and departure to Nibbāna-dhātu.

**Comparison with Modern Understanding**

This reactive layer finds clear echoes in contemporary psychology and neuroscience:

- **Emotional Reactivity and Limbic Hijack** --- Daniel Goleman's concept of "amygdala hijack" describes how intense stimuli trigger the limbic system (amygdala, hippocampus) before the prefrontal cortex can respond rationally. This mirrors reactive citta: emotional surge dominates knowing, bypassing discernment, leading to impulsive action or overwhelm.

- **Attachment Theory & Emotional Regulation** --- John Bowlby and modern attachment research show how early relational patterns create habitual emotional reactions (anxious, avoidant, disorganized). Reactive citta reflects this: mind leans toward (anxious clinging) or pushes away (avoidant withdrawal) based on conditioned templates, with little freedom from the pull-push dynamic.

- **Neuroscience: Salience Network & Emotional Reactivity** --- The salience network (anterior insula, anterior cingulate cortex) detects emotionally relevant stimuli and switches attention/resources. In untrained minds, this network over-dominates, keeping citta bound to reactive valence (threat/reward). This corresponds to the push-pull agitation at this layer.

- **Trauma Responses (Fight/Flight/Freeze/Fawn)** --- Polyvagal theory (Stephen Porges) and trauma models show how perceived threat activates autonomic responses: fight (anger), flight (fear/anxiety), freeze (dissociation), fawn (appeasement). Reactive citta encompasses these survival-based emotional surges, where citta is enslaved to threat detection and protection.

Higher seeing at this level reveals:

- Reactive citta is the direct outcome of vedanā and volitional nutriment.

- It conditions the strongest emotional turbulence and push-pull in saṃsāra.

- Yet it is conditioned, impermanent, and not true self --- never a source of true stability or knowing.

- The true citta --- luminous at its core, deathless-capable --- is never identical with this agitated, reactive state; it uses clear seeing to loosen emotional binding and prepare for independence (anissito).

The practitioner abides in this reactive field, seeing its emotional surges and volitional leanings without grasping attraction or fighting aversion. As understanding matures here, identification with reactive states as "me" begins to weaken. The door opens naturally to the next layer: patterned (habitual) citta, where emotional reactions solidify into fixed tendencies and identity structures.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 8.5 Level 3 --- Patterned (Habitual) Citta

As citta-anupassanā penetrates beyond the reactive surges, citta discloses its next layer: the patterned or habitual level. Here, fleeting emotional reactions solidify into stable, repeating structures --- tendencies, habits, and identity formations that persist across time.

It manifests as:

- habitual emotional tendencies (chronic anxiety, recurring anger, persistent desire patterns)

- fixed personality traits and behavioral loops

- repetitive thought patterns and self-narratives

- conditioned identity structures ("I am this kind of person," "this is how I always respond")

Characteristics include:

- repetitive cycles of reaction that feel automatic and "natural"

- emotional and volitional habits that shape perception and action

- personality formation and self-image solidifying

- citta operating through predictable, conditioned loops rather than fresh response

At this layer, citta is no longer just reacting moment-to-moment; it has become structured by past volition, conditioning, and repeated experience. Habits form grooves: fear-based avoidance becomes a default stance, desire-based grasping becomes a chronic leaning, anger flares along familiar triggers. Identity embeds deeply --- citta begins to experience itself as "I am anxious," "I am ambitious," "I am wounded," carrying these patterns as defining characteristics. The luminous core remains veiled beneath layers of habit and narrative.

Through citta-anupassanā, the practitioner abides inside this patterned field and sees it directly as it is --- without trying to change habits by force or suppress patterns. Higher seeing reveals:

- patterned states arise dependent on repeated volitional nutriment (cetanā) and emotional reinforcement

- habits condition perception: the same stimulus is filtered through the same lens, producing the same reaction

- no permanent "I" owns these patterns --- only conditioned saṅkhāras (formations) repeating through momentum

- citta at this level is worldly, bound to habitual becoming and identity-clinging

As this clear seeing stabilizes:

- the automatic repetition becomes visible instead of invisible

- the mind begins to recognize its own grooves rather than being carried by them

- identification with "this is who I am" or "this is how I always feel" starts to loosen

Even at this patterned layer, the practitioner directly understands: patterned citta belongs entirely to the domain of conditioned arising and ceasing. It can never be the luminous, deathless-capable core. It can never serve as the final standing-place or lasting home for the knowing faculty capable of purification and departure to Nibbāna-dhātu.

**Comparison with Modern Understanding**

This patterned layer aligns closely with contemporary insights:

- **Habit Loops & Basal Ganglia (Neuroscience)** --- Charles Duhigg and neuroscientific models describe habits as cue → routine → reward loops encoded in the basal ganglia. Patterned citta reflects this: a sensory cue triggers a habitual emotional/volitional response (routine), reinforced by temporary relief or gratification (reward), creating self-perpetuating circuits.

- **Schema Therapy & Core Beliefs (Psychology)** --- Jeffrey Young's schema therapy identifies early maladaptive schemas (e.g., defectiveness, abandonment, entitlement) that form rigid patterns of perception, emotion, and behavior. These schemas filter experience, just as patterned citta imposes habitual lenses on the world, sustaining identity narratives.

- **Attachment Styles & Internal Working Models (Developmental Psychology)** --- Bowlby/Ainsworth's internal working models show how early relationships create lasting templates for relating (secure, anxious, avoidant, disorganized). Patterned citta carries these models as emotional/relational habits --- "I expect rejection," "I must cling to avoid abandonment" --- driving repetitive patterns in adulthood.

- **Cognitive Biases & Confirmation Bias** --- The mind selectively attends to information that confirms existing beliefs and ignores contradictions. Patterned citta operates through this bias: once a self-narrative forms ("I am unworthy"), citta habitually interprets experience to reinforce it, perpetuating the pattern.

Higher seeing at this level reveals:

- Patterned citta is the direct outcome of repeated volitional nutriment and emotional reinforcement.

- It conditions the strongest habitual identity-clinging and repetitive becoming in saṃsāra.

- Yet it is conditioned, impermanent, and impersonal --- never a source of true stability or knowing.

- The true citta --- luminous at its core, deathless-capable --- is never identical with these habitual patterns or identity structures; it uses clear seeing to loosen conditioning and prepare for independence (anissito).

The practitioner abides in this patterned field, seeing its repetitive loops and identity-forming tendencies without grasping habit as "me" or fighting pattern as "enemy." As understanding matures here, identification with patterned states as "who I am" begins to weaken profoundly.

**Letting Go**

The key to dissolving patterned citta lies in letting go --- not through suppression or forceful change, but through wise non-clinging and clear seeing. When the practitioner observes a habitual pattern (e.g., chronic fear, recurring anger, attachment to self-image) without feeding it with identification or reaction, the momentum weakens. Letting go here means:

- not believing the pattern as "true self" or permanent

- not resisting it with aversion (which creates new patterns)

- not indulging it with craving (which reinforces the loop)

Instead, the mind simply knows the pattern as conditioned arising --- arising due to past volition and reinforcement, persisting through habit, fading when unfed. This non-grasping, non-rejecting attitude starves the groove. Over time, the pattern loses power; the groove shallows; citta regains freedom to respond freshly rather than repeat mechanically.

Letting go is the practical expression of nibbida and virāga (disenchantment and fading of attachment) at this level. It is not denial of the pattern, but non-dependence on it --- allowing citta to stand independent of habitual identity. Through sustained letting go, the patterned layer dissolves naturally, revealing the luminous core beneath.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 8.6 Level 4 --- Conceptual / Self-Identity Citta 

(also Mental-Conceptual Citta or Ego-Identity Layer)

As citta-anupassanā matures beyond the patterned/habitual grooves, citta enters its conceptual and self-identity layer. Here the mind actively constructs, sustains, and clings to a coherent sense of "I" through interpretation, self-referencing, narrative-building, and identification.

It manifests as:

- self-referential thought ("I am experiencing this," "this is happening to me")

- assigning personal meaning and ownership to experience ("this feeling means I am loved/rejected," "this sensation is my pain/my pleasure")

- weaving disparate experiences into a continuous personal story ("my life is like this," "I am this kind of person")

- defending and polishing the constructed identity ("I must protect this image of myself," "I deserve this because of who I am")

Characteristics include:

- emergence of a felt "I" at the center of knowing

- conceptual scaffolding of identity through roles, traits, history, and beliefs

- reflection used not for liberation, but for self-justification and self-protection

- citta turning inward to fabricate and maintain "me" rather than to see clearly

This layer is the direct continuation and culmination of the conceptual processes already begun in the earlier fields:

- In the **body** (Chapter 6 Level 4 --- Mental-Functional Body), the mind first constructs a conceptual image and boundaries of form ("this is my shape," "this is where I end and the world begins").

- In **feeling** (Chapter 7 Level 4 --- Conceptualized Feeling), raw tone is interpreted and overlaid with meaning ("this pleasure is good/mine," "this pain is bad/unfair"), supplying the emotional valence that feeds identity.

- Here in **citta**, the mind takes these mental images of body and conceptualized tones of feeling and actively synthesizes them into a coherent, owned narrative of self: "I am the body that feels this pleasure/pain," "I am the one who deserves/does not deserve," "this story of suffering/success is my story."

This is the decisive stage where the **false self** (the eating bird, ahaṅkāra, sakkāya-diṭṭhi) is most explicitly assembled and defended. The conceptualized body provides the "container" ("this form is me"), conceptualized feeling provides the "content" ("these tones define my worth"), and identity-forming citta provides the "storyteller" --- turning raw experience into a persistent "I" with biography, motives, rights, wounds, and goals. Cravings and aversions from earlier layers are now rationalized and protected: "I am cherished," "I am offended," "I need this pleasure because I am deprived," "I must avoid that pain because I am fragile/superior." The luminous true citta is veiled most thickly here --- not by raw reactivity or blind habit, but by the sophisticated narrative that feels most convincingly like "me."

Through citta-anupassanā, the practitioner abides inside this self-identity field and sees it directly as it is --- without grasping the constructed "I" as ultimate truth or fighting the narrative as enemy. Higher seeing reveals:

- conceptual identity arises dependent on prior body-image and feeling-interpretation

- self-referencing and narrative-building are conditioned by volition, habit, and ignorance

- no permanent, independent self exists behind the story --- only saṅkhāras (formations) weaving a sense of continuity

- citta at this level is still fully within loka, deeply bound to self-clinging and worldly becoming

As this clear seeing stabilizes:

- the mind begins to notice the act of self-storytelling itself

- the constructed "I" loses automatic credibility

- identification with "this is who I really am" or "this story defines me" starts to loosen

Even at this conceptual/self-identity layer, the practitioner directly understands: this citta belongs to the domain of conditioned arising and ceasing. It can never be the luminous, deathless-capable core. It can never serve as the ultimate standing-place or lasting home for the knowing faculty capable of complete purification and departure to Nibbāna-dhātu.

**Letting Go as a Way to Dissolve the False Self**

The key to dissolving this layer lies in letting go --- not denying thought or narrative, but releasing identification with the constructed "I." When the practitioner observes a self-referential story ("I am unworthy," "I must achieve to be safe") without believing it as ultimate reality or defending it, the momentum of ego-fabrication weakens. Letting go here means:

- not taking the narrative as truth or permanent self

- not resisting the story with aversion (which creates new self-defense)

- not indulging it with craving (which reinforces the identity)

Instead, the mind simply knows the conceptual self as conditioned arising --- useful in the world, yet impermanent and impersonal. This non-grasping, non-rejecting attitude starves the false self. Over time, the narrative loses solidity; the "I" story thins; the luminous true citta begins to stand forth unobscured.

Higher seeing at this level reveals:

- Conceptual / self-identity citta is the culmination of body-image and feeling-interpretation into a fabricated "I."

- It conditions the deepest clinging to personal identity and worldly becoming.

- Yet it is conditioned, impermanent, and not true self (anatta) --- never the true self.

- The luminous citta --- deathless-capable --- is never identical with this constructed self or ego; it uses clear seeing to dismantle the false identity and prepare for independence (anissito).

The practitioner abides in this conceptual field, seeing its narrative-building and self-referencing without grasping "I am this story" or recoiling from the constructed self. As understanding matures here, identification with the false self as "me" weakens profoundly. The door opens naturally to the next layer: collected (unified) citta, where the mind begins to unify and stabilize beyond fragmentation and identity-clinging.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 8.7 Level 5 --- Divine Identity / Brahmanic Citta

With strong development of practice --- through sustained sīla, samādhi, and insight --- citta reaches the Divine Identity layer. Here, fragmentation gives way to unification by aligning with the brahmanic field, and the mind lifts beyond the dominance of the sensory world.

It manifests as:

- citta becoming unified

- attention stabilizing naturally

- fragmentation and scattering dissolving

Characteristics include:

- deep calm

- luminous clarity

- sustained focus and inner coherence

- powerful stability that no longer wavers with external contact or inner turbulence

This is the stage where citta steps out of the worldly self-identity constructed in the previous layer (the conceptual/ego-self citta built from sensory contact, interpreted feeling, and self-referential narrative). Through disciplined training in brahmacariya (celibate life, withdrawal from sensual desire craving and worldly entanglement) and cultivation of the brahmavihāra (mettā, karuṇā, muditā, upekkhā), citta begins to align with the Brahma realms --- our ancestral divine source. At higher refinement of citta, it becomes calm, expansive, and balanced, aligned with higher, value-based qualities. This includes:

- mettā (kindness) --- pure goodwill extended without attachment or expectation

- karuṇā (compassion) --- active response to suffering without domination or pity

- muditā (rejoicing in manussa) --- Brahmic joy that celebrates the virtue, success, and welfare of manussa, the race of man

- upekkhā (transcendental liberation) --- aligned with transcendental liberation, steady balance when dealing with human affairs that stands beyond favor, fear, aversion, or partiality

In the prior layer, the false self was fabricated: a worldly "I" woven from body-image, emotional tone, and conceptual ownership ("this is me," "this is mine"). Here, that worldly identity is gradually released --- not through rejection or suppression, but through non-clinging and higher alignment. Citta moves from identifying with conditioned, sensory-derived stories to resonating with divine, value-based qualities: pure goodwill, compassionate response, rejoicing in virtue, and transcendental equanimity.

This shift to divine identity is a crucial step toward discovering the true self --- the luminous citta rooted in the Brahmanic source, deathless-capable and unbound by worldly becoming. It is the vital bridge: the mind becomes powerful and stable, lifted beyond sensory dominance to join the divine field resonant with the Brahma heavens. The luminous core shines more clearly; the veil of worldly clinging thins dramatically.

The brahmavihāra are not optional ornaments; they are the essential training that enables this transition. They:

- counteract the poisons that bind citta to the lower layers

- align citta with the Brahma realms --- pure, non-sensual, value-based states

- create the ethical and emotional spaciousness needed for deeper insight without ego-rebound

- serve as preserved world-transcending support (as emphasized earlier: letting go of the worldly does not mean letting go of everything --- brahmavihāra and noble values remain as the luminous orientation until final release)

Without this Brahmanic unification and divine alignment, the mind may become dry, brittle, or subtly arrogant in its "detachment." With it, citta gains the stability and purity to face ultimate truths without recoil or clinging. This layer is therefore the decisive step-stone --- the collected, divine field that allows citta to stand firm as it approaches complete purification.

Gautama, the Supreme Arahant repeatedly identified himself with this Brahma lineage, declaring in the Pali Canon:

"Ahamasmi, bhikkhave, brāhmaṇo yācayogo sadā payatapāṇi antimadehadharo anuttaro bhisakko sallakatto. Tassa me tumhe puttā orasā mukhato jātā dhammajā dhammanimmitā dhammadāyādā, no āmisadāyādā."

"Bhikkhus, I am a brāhmaṇa, committed to charity, always open-handed, bearing my final body, a healer, a surgeon. You are my rightful children, born of my mouth, born of my Dhamma, created by my Dhamma, heirs in my Dhamma, not in material things." (Itivuttaka 4.100, Brāhmaṇa-dhamma-yāga Sutta)

In Dhammapada, the entire Brāhmaṇa Vagga (Chapter 26) celebrates this noble identity:

"Sabbasaṃyojanaṃ chetvā, yo ve na paritassati; Saṅgātigaṃ visaṃyuttaṃ, tam ahaṃ brūmi Brāhmaṇaṃ." (397)

"He who has cut off all bonds, who does not tremble, who has overcome attachments and is unbound --- him I call a brāhmana."

Higher seeing at this level reveals:

- Divine Identity / Brahmanic citta is the direct outcome of brahmacariya and brahmavihāra cultivation and deep samādhi.

- It marks the shift from worldly self-identity to divine identity --- a vital step toward uncovering the true self rooted in the Brahmanic source.

- It conditions profound stability and value-alignment on the path.

- Yet it is conditioned and impermanent as long as it stays in the world.

### 8.8 Level 6 --- Purified Citta (Arahant Level)

At the highest level of conditioned experience, citta reaches its complete purification --- the layer where all defilements are eradicated and the luminous core stands fully revealed.

It manifests as:

- all defilements absent

- no attachment remains

- no identification persists

Characteristics include:

- complete non-reactivity

- absence of **rāga** (worldly craving and attachment), **dosa** (survival instincts of fear and anger), **moha** (ignorance and delusion, not knowing the liberation path)

- full independence from the world (anissito ca viharati)

- luminous, unobstructed knowing that abides without leaning on any conditioned phenomenon

Citta is fully purified and liberated from worldly conditioning. No trace of **rāga**, **dosa**, or **moha** remains to obscure or agitate it. There is no longer any false worldly "I" to protect, no craving to pursue, no aversion to resist. The mind stands in its original luminosity --- deathless-capable, radiant, and utterly free.

This is the arahant level: the complete uprooting of the three poisons, the fulfillment of the path, the realization of vimutti (liberation from the world). Citta no longer generates new worldly becoming (bhava); no fuel remains for saṃsāra. The false self --- constructed across the previous layers from sensory binding, reactive surges, habitual patterns, and conceptual identity --- has been seen through and dissolved. What remains is the pure citta: luminous at its core, not dependent on the world, ready for final departure when the last residue of kamma ceases.

The practitioner directly understands: purified citta is the culmination of the path within conditioned existence. It is the luminous core freed from all worldly support, deathless in essence, the true self.

The practitioner abides in this purified citta, seeing its complete non-reactivity, absence of defilement, and full independence from the world. Citta stands luminous, unbound, and prepared for the final departure.

This completes the six layers of citta. Through citta-anupassanā, the practitioner has seen mind in mind from sensory-bound reactivity to purified independence, stands free, no longer leaning on any conditioned layer.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 8.9 Closing Reflection

Citta is the central axis of the path --- the luminous faculty that knows body, experiences feeling, and engages mental phenomena. It begins scattered and reactive, bound to sensory input and worldly conditioning.

Through direct higher seeing (citta-anupassanā) and the progressive training of sīla, samādhi, and paññā, a transformation unfolds across the layers:

Early

- reactive and dependent --- pulled by contact, feeling, and impulse

Middle

- aware and stabilizing --- beginning to see patterns, loosen automaticity

Advanced

- unified and divine --- collected, aligned with brahmavihāra values, resonant with Brahma realms

Final

- purified and free --- all defilements eradicated, luminous core fully revealed

Citta and vedanā are closely linked: feeling influences citta; citta reacts to feeling. Through practice, citta sees feeling clearly --- reaction weakens, grasping fades. Thus citta becomes independent of feeling, no longer leaning on tone for support or identity.

Samādhi is essential. It provides stability, clarity, and strength. Without samādhi, citta remains scattered and insight stays shallow. With samādhi, citta gains the power to penetrate reality --- unifying fragmentation, lifting beyond sensory dominance, and supporting the emergence of purification.

Purification occurs when defilements are seen clearly, worldly attachment loses its basis, and identification with the world dissolves. This is not suppression --- it is release through understanding. When citta is purified:

- it does not grasp anything worldly

- it does not resist but let go of the worldly

- it does not identify with the world

Thus no worldly clinging arises. This fulfills the canonical expression: "na ca kiñci loke upādiyati" --- does not cling to anything in the world.

Purified citta is ready to go to Nibbāna-dhātu. Before final departure, it may resonate with qualities of Nibbāna-dhātu --- peace (Santi), purity (Suddha), stability (Dhuva), bliss (Sukha beyond sense), deathlessness (Amata), and supreme stilling (upasama). These are conditioned reflections --- glimpses of the Deathless --- yet they point unmistakably to the true home.

At this culmination, citta is anissito --- independent from the world. No support is sought in conditioned existence. No new becoming arises. When citta is fully purified, it is ready for Vimutti --- liberation from the world, complete release from saṃsāra.

Citta begins scattered and reactive. Through practice:

- it becomes stable

- then unified and divine

- then purified

Finally: it stands free, independent, and ready to depart. From this independence: the Deathless is realized. The luminous citta, deathless-capable, no longer leans on any residue of the world. The path is complete.

![](media/image13.jpeg){width="4.545138888888889in" height="3.0319444444444446in"}

## 9. The Structure of Dhammā

***From Mental Constructions to the Reflection of the Deathless***

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 9.1 Dhammā as the Field of Mental Phenomena

In Satipaṭṭhāna, the fourth foundation is:

**dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati** "abides higher-seeing mental phenomena in mental phenomena"

This grammatical structure carries operational force, as in the previous fields:

- **dhammesu** (locative) --- within / inside the field of phenomena

- **dhamma-anupassī** --- higher-seeing / supreme-seeing phenomena

- **viharati** --- abides / dwells continuously in that mode

The practitioner does not stand apart analyzing dhammā as objects or concepts. Awareness penetrates and abides inside the living field of mental phenomena, seeing dhammā in dhammā as layered structures from within the field of dhamma.

The term dhammā is often misunderstood. It does not refer only to teachings, doctrines, or ethical principles. Here, dhammā means all mental phenomena arising within the field of citta --- the total spectrum of mental events, structures, and patterns that unfold from the citta. This includes:

- emotions (anger, joy, fear, compassion)

- thoughts (ideas, reasoning, interpretations)

- concepts (beliefs, views, frameworks)

- patterns (habits, tendencies, repetitions)

- volitions (intentions, impulses, decisions)

- structures of identity (self-images, roles, narratives)

Dhammā is the total field of mental phenomena --- the dynamic content that citta knows, engages, and is shaped by. It is inseparable from the other fields: dhammā arise upon the foundation of kāya (body) and vedanā (feeling), and they in turn condition citta\'s states. But in dhamma-anupassanā, the practitioner focuses on dhammā as they manifest from citta--- seeing how they construct perception, identity, and the entire "world" of experience.

Dhammā can be understood in functional groups:

- **Emotional Dhammā**: anger, desire, fear, attachment --- intense affective formations that surge and subside.

- **Cognitive Dhammā**: thoughts, reasoning, interpretations --- the mental processes that assign meaning and logic.

- **Imaginal Dhammā**: images, memories, projections --- the visionary or symbolic content that fills the mind\'s eye.

- **Volitional Dhammā**: intentions, decisions, impulses --- the directional energy that propels action and karma.

- **Habitual Dhammā**: tendencies, conditioning, repetition patterns --- the grooves that sustain automatic responses.

- **Worldly Self (Ego) Identity Dhammā**: self-image, roles, narratives --- the constructions that form the false self (ahaṅkāra, the eating bird).

- **Value-Aligned Divine Identity Dhammā** (Brahmavihāra): mettā (kindness), karuṇā (compassion), muditā (Brahmic joy), upekkhā (transcendental equanimity) --- refined states that stabilize citta and align with the Brahma source.

- **Nibbana-dhatu Resonant Dhammā**: reflections of Nibbāna qualities --- peace (Santi), blissful (Sugata), purity (Suddha), stability (Dhuva), undecaying (Ajajjara), deathless (Amata), permanent (Dhuva), truth (Sacca), island (Dīpa), refuge (Leṇa), protection (Tāṇa), Final Destination (Parāyana) --- non-binding phenomena that point beyond the world.

Through dhammā, the false self (worldly self, ego) and the "world" are constructed: perception is interpreted, meaning assigned, identity formed, attachment sustained. The practitioner sees how dhammā arise, stabilize, and dissolve --- not random, but structured by conditioning, volition, and ignorance.

The purpose of dhamma-anupassanā is radical: to see dhammā so directly and completely that dependence upon them as true self, refuge, or support ceases. This clear higher seeing progressively frees citta from the entire field of mental phenomena, preparing it to abide independent (anissito) from the world and depart toward Nibbāna-dhātu, which stands utterly beyond all dhamma (not only mental phenomena but also all phenomena/existence in the world) and all worlds.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### 9.2 Dhamma-anupassanā --- Mental Phenomena in Mental Phenomena as Layered Structured Processes

The canonical instruction is precise:

**dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati**

"abides higher-seeing mental phenomena in mental phenomena"

This higher seeing means:

- observing mental phenomena in their layered structures

- within their own arising

- without conceptual overlay

The practitioner does not analyze dhammā conceptually, categorize them as objects, or stand apart reflecting on them. Instead, awareness enters the field of dhammā itself and abides within it. Dhammā are not random events or isolated occurrences. They form structured processes that arise, stabilize, and dissolve according to conditions:

- patterns of thought (repetitive sequences of ideas or logic)

- emotional formations (sustained affective states like anger or compassion)

- habitual tendencies (conditioned impulses and responses)

- identity constructions (self-images, roles, narratives)

These structures unfold directly from the layered citta: sensory-bound citta gives rise to basic impulses, reactive citta to emotional formations, patterned citta to habitual tendencies, conceptual citta to identity constructions, collected divine citta to value-aligned patterns, and purified citta to nibbana-dhatu resonant dhammā.

Through direct higher seeing, their nature becomes clear: dhammā are conditioned, impermanent, and not true self --- arising dependent on citta\'s state, stabilizing through repetition and volition, dissolving when unfed.
